| **[ReSeg: Retraining-free segmentation of abdominal organs via generalizable semantic representation learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29090v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Abdominal organ segmentation is essential in modern medical applications. While existing deep learning approaches demonstrate promising performance, their heavy reliance on large-scale image data raises concerns over generalization capability and privacy risks. This motivates the development of generalizable segmentation training paradigms based on domain-randomized image synthesis, facilitating generalization across unseen real image domains. However, existing paradigms either suffer from limited segmentation performance when applied to abdominal organs, or require dataset-specific finetuning. To address these limitations, we propose ReSeg, a novel two-stage retraining-free framework for domain-agnostic zero-shot abdominal organ segmentation. At the first stage, our organ-agnostic synthetic data-driven pretraining approach exploits semantically and visually diverse synthetic images generated by a dedicated data engine to optimize a Generalizable Semantic Representation Network (GSRNet). The network is thereby capable of learning semantic features robust against variations in anatomical structure, imaging contrast, resolution and spatial context, which provide universal representation capabilities that serve as a foundation for segmentation learning. Subsequently, in the second stage, we continue to train an Organ-specific Encoder-decoder Segmentation Network (OESNet), whose encoder is initialized with the pretrained weights. This training process is facilitated by another dedicated data engine, which generates diversified visual images while strictly retaining the inherent spatial layout of abdominal organs. Comprehensive evaluation on seven abdominal organ segmentation datasets across three different modalities including CT, MRI, and PET demonstrates that ReSeg consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods while eliminating the need for real training images.</p></details> |  |
| **[Zero-Shot Cross-City Generalization in End-to-End Autonomous Driving: Self-Supervised versus Supervised Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11417v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>End-to-end autonomous driving models are typically trained on multi-city datasets using supervised ImageNet-pretrained backbones, yet their ability to generalize to unseen cities remains largely unexamined. When training and evaluation data are geographically mixed, models may implicitly rely on city-specific cues, masking failure modes that would occur under real-world domain shifts when generalizing to new locations. In this work, we formulate zero-shot cross-city transfer as a controlled representation-level stress test for end-to-end autonomous driving and ask how visual pretraining affects transfer behavior under geographic domain shift. We conduct a comprehensive study by integrating self-supervised backbones I-JEPA, DINOv2, and MAE into planning frameworks. We evaluate performance under strict geographic splits on nuScenes in the open-loop setting and on NAVSIM in the closed-loop evaluation protocol. Our experiments reveal a substantial generalization gap when transferring models across cities with different road topologies, traffic conventions, and visual environments. In open-loop evaluation, a supervised backbone exhibits severe degradation when transferring between cities, yet some domain-specific self-supervised methods can substantially reduce both displacement and collision degradation. In closed-loop evaluation, self-supervised pretraining improves average out-of-distribution PDMS in several single-city training settings. Our results provide empirical evidence that representation learning influences the robustness of cross-city planning and motivate zero-shot geographic transfer as an important stress test for evaluating end-to-end autonomous driving systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Depth-guided Geometric Adaptation for Few-shot Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28856v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Few-shot image classification (FSIC) frequently suffers from visual ambiguity, where background clutter and RGB appearance similarities hinder discriminative feature matching in data-scarce settings. To mitigate this, we propose DAFIC, a geometry-guided framework that incorporates monocular depth into representation learning, episodic adaptation, and dense support-query matching. DAFIC first learns geometry-aware token representations through RGB-D self-supervised pre-training. Subsequently, the Depth-guided Geometric Adaptation (DGA) module uses depth-derived geometry to refine patch representations with geometry-enriched class context while suppressing background interference. Spatial Bidirectional Patch Matching (SBiM) then performs bidirectional dense matching on the refined features, reducing sensitivity to spurious correspondences and background clutter. Extensive experiments on four benchmarks show that DAFIC achieves strong performance against existing methods, with ablation and robustness analyses supporting the effectiveness of coupling geometry-conditioned episodic adaptation with bidirectional matching for few-shot visual recognition.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This work is accepted by The IEEE/CVF Winter Conference on Applications of Computer Vision (WACV) 2027</p></details> |
| **[Spatially Informed and Direction Sensitive Representation Learning for Spatial Transcriptomics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28579v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cellular states are shaped by both intrinsic gene expression programs and the spatial microenvironment in which cells reside. Spatial transcriptomics makes this context measurable, but effective representation learning requires capturing both spatially informative molecular signals and intercellular organization. Existing approaches address these challenges only partially: conventional gene selection can overlook spatially structured signals, while methods for encoding cell-cell relationships often either reduce a cell's microenvironment to proximity alone, discarding direction, or rely on absolute coordinates tied to an arbitrary tissue frame. Consequently, cells with similar neighbor distances but distinct spatial organization may receive overly similar representations, despite experiencing different biological contexts that shape their cellular states. We introduce Tessera, a spatial representation learning framework that addresses both molecular selection and cellular context. Tessera combines spatially informed gene selection with a geometric inductive bias that contextualizes each cell within its local tissue environment. By modeling relative cell-cell relationships, Tessera is translation-invariant while preserving direction, allowing neighboring cells to contribute differently according to their spatial context and cellular state. Across held-out tissues and technologies, Tessera outperforms existing spatial foundation models in representation quality and generalizes zero-shot to contexts up to $16\times$ larger than those seen during training.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This manuscript has been accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on AI for Drug Discovery: Bridging the Translation Gap (AI4DD)</p></details> |
| **[Multi-Marginal Inverse Optimal Transport for Contrastive Learning Via Explicit Anchor-Positive-Negative Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33741v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inverse Optimal Transport (OT) based methods for representation learning learn representations such that the global OT coupling between a pair of data marginals in the representation space, concentrates on the positive pairs. This is in contrast to previous methods that primarily focused on pairwise matching. However, these methods $\textit{DO NOT}$ utilize negative pairs and hence are not truly contrastive in their approach. We show that this leads to issues of dimensional collapse and hence degraded downstream performance. To alleviate this, we develop a novel multi-marginal (MM) inverse OT (IOT) contrastive learning (CL) approach called Neg-MMIOT-CL, which learns representations such that the global multi-marginal OT (MMOT) coupling between a triple of data marginals, with respect to a carefully designed ground-cost between triplets of data points in the representation space, concentrates on the anchor-positive-negative $\textit{triplets}$. For a latent class model, we empirically show that Neg-MMIOT-CL alleviates dimensional collapse. Furthermore, for a specific choice of ground cost for all triplets in representation space, we prove that the optimal representation configuration for Neg-MMIOT-CL exhibits equiangular property for within-class and across-class representations, which translates to Neural-Collapse when the representation dimension is larger than the number of classes minus one -- a result that is $\textit{previously established only}$ for pairwise contrastive learning methods. Finally, we propose Neg-IOT-CL-PushPull, that is a computationally efficient alternative to Neg-MMIOT-CL, alleviating the high cost of computing MMOT plans needed during implementation. We apply these methods on both synthetic and real-world datasets and show significant improvements over existing OT-based contrastive learning methods.</p></details> |  |
| **[Touch-JEPA: Tactile Representation Learning from Hand-Object Video with Limited Tactile Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28519v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding contact and tactile magnitude in hand-object interaction is a key step toward physically intelligent systems, yet learning such representations is limited by the cost and restricted coverage of tactile measurements. We present Touch-JEPA, a framework for learning tactile representations from hand-object videos with limited tactile supervision. We first introduce a self-supervised pretraining objective that learns from video alone without tactile supervision. Using video-derived hand geometry, Touch-JEPA predicts masked visual features at corresponding hand-surface locations from spatial and temporal context, encouraging the representation to capture latent contact dynamics. We then extend this formulation to joint representation learning, where masked prediction is applied to all videos while tactile supervision is available only for a labeled subset, grounding the shared representation in measured contact and magnitude. With self-supervised pretraining alone, simple supervised readouts on frozen Touch-JEPA features outperform supervised baselines in contact discrimination and tactile magnitude estimation across three human-hand benchmarks, and transfer to robotic hands without robot tactile training data. Joint learning with limited tactile supervision further improves contact and magnitude estimation on the human-hand benchmarks. Together, these results show that hand-object video can provide scalable self-supervision for tactile representation learning, while limited tactile measurements provide complementary physical grounding.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://touch-jepa.github.io</p></details> |
| **[Exploring Temporal Context-Aware Physical Grounding in Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28483v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning latent dynamics is fundamental to building action-conditioned world models for planning. Among existing families, Joint Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) offer a natural framework by modeling transitions directly in latent space rather than at the pixel level. However, two issues remain in JEPA-based world models: (i) physical grounding lacks explicit aggregation of temporal context and (ii) action planning always overly relies on generic sampling distributions. To this end, we propose $\mathcal{G}$-JEPA, which incorporates temporal context into physical grounding for latent world models and couples the learned models with prior-guided action planning. Specifically, during learning, we incorporate a shared causal temporal module that aggregates temporal context within observation and prediction sequences under physical state supervision, grounding both in task-relevant physical states. When planning, we further introduce action inference through stepwise conditioning of a generative action distribution on the current and goal latent representations together with their evolving discrepancy, yielding anchored proposals that guide model-based online refinement. Together, physical state supervision guides temporal representation learning, while the action prior guides search through the learned world model. Experiments demonstrate improved planning performance on manipulation tasks, including tasks with increasing temporal separation between initial and goal observations. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/myl11045/G-JEPA.</p></details> |  |
| **[Causal Discovery for Linear Models with Latent Confounding via Multi-domain Identifiable Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28134v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Causal discovery with latent confounding is particularly challenging, but a common setting in real-world data. In this work, we focus on linear causal models with latent confounding, represented by acyclic directed mixed graphs (ADMGs). Existing methods that fully identify ADMGs typically assume non-Gaussianity or graphical constraints, such as bow-freeness. In contrast, we propose Linear ADMG Learning (LinADMG), a framework for learning ADMGs for linear causal models through identifiable representation learning. Rather than relying on non-Gaussianity or bow-freeness, LinADMG leverages multi-domain settings to identify the noise variables. We show that the identifiability of the noise variables is sufficient for recovering all direct causal relations, and that, under an additional latent-confounding no-cancellation condition, LinADMG can identify the whole ADMG. We implement LinADMG with four identifiable representation learning approaches from the literature and validate the methods on simulated and semisynthetic datasets.</p></details> | 29 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Robust Representation Learning for Spiking Neural Networks based on Conditional Information Bottleneck](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28110v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) have shown great potential for low-power neuromorphic computing due to their event-driven processing and spatiotemporal sparsity, but they remain vulnerable to input perturbations, hardware-intrinsic noise, and adversarial attacks in real-world deployment. We attribute this vulnerability partly to input-specific hidden representations learned by standard SNNs trained with conventional cross-entropy loss, as our information-plane analysis empirically shows that substantial input-related information can still be retained after label-predictive information becomes stable. To address this issue, we propose SCIB, a simple yet effective robust learning objective for SNNs based on the Conditional Information Bottleneck. Different from conventional variational IB methods, SCIB replaces the mutual information compression term $I(X;S)$ with the conditional mutual information $I(X;S|Y)$ and derives a tractable variational upper bound. Moreover, it introduces a learnable class-conditional prior instead of a label-agnostic isotropic Gaussian prior, improving the expressiveness of variational compression and encouraging class-structured representations when combined with the supervised classification objective. Systematic experiments on Fashion-MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100 under external environmental noise, neuromorphic hardware noise, and adversarial attacks show that SCIB improves robustness over standard SNNs and IB-based baselines under most evaluated noise conditions, improves adversarial accuracy under the evaluated attacks without using adversarial training, and reduces the SynOps-based single-sample inference energy consumption by 24.9% relative to an unconstrained SNN baseline.</p></details> | 17 pages |
| **[Learning Tactile-Aware Hand Representations from Human Hand-Object Interaction Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27995v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tactile representation learning for dexterous manipulation often requires substantial robot-side data collection, limiting its scalability across different hands and sensors. We explore whether large-scale human hand-object interaction (HOI) data can provide a useful prior for robotic tactile learning. To this end, we propose HuTact, a framework that pretrains tactile-aware hand representations from human HOI data without requiring real tactile signals during pretraining. HuTact transfers the interaction structure learned from human hand motions to robotic tactile observations and can be further adapted with a small amount of unlabeled robot interaction data. We evaluate HuTact on tactile perception and manipulation tasks, including grasp prediction, slip detection, object classification, and tactile-conditioned policy learning. Across these tasks, human HOI pretraining improves data efficiency and provides a strong initialization for downstream learning. Notably, with HOI pretraining followed by midtraining on unlabeled robot play data, HuTact achieves 88.26% Macro-F1 on grasp prediction using only 25\% of the labeled downstream data, comparable to training from scratch on the full labeled dataset (88.23%). These results suggest that human interaction data can serve as a scalable complementary source for robotic tactile representation learning.</p></details> | 9 pages, 4 figures |
| **[HypTI: Hyperbolic Geometry-Aware Multimodal Feature Fusion Framework for Dementia Detection Based on Tabular-Image Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27941v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI-assisted dementia detection usually requires multimodal fusion of diverse data, such as clinical tabular assessments and neuroimaging data. However, most existing approaches perform feature fusion in Euclidean space. This overlooks the geometric structure of representation, including hierarchical organization in clinical assessments and multi-scale anatomical patterns in neuroimaging data. Recent studies have proved hyperbolic space provides a natural geometry for modelling hierarchical and multi-scale structures due to its exponentially expanding space. Motivated by these, we propose HypTI, a hyperbolic geometry-aware multimodal fusion framework for dementia detection based on clinical tabular assessments and MRI scans. HypTI extends hyperbolic representation learning to an underexplored tabular-MRI setting, jointly modelling clinical tabular, MRI scans, and cross-modal interactions in hyperbolic space. Specifically, clinical tabular variables are represented through learnable Lorentz prototypes to capture hierarchical relationships among assessments, while MRI scans are encoded using ROI-guided multi-scale representation learning to capture anatomical patterns across brain regions and spatial scales in Lorentz space. The multimodal representations are integrated through an adaptive fusion module generates subject-specific scale and shift coefficients to dynamically modulate and integrate the multimodal embeddings, enabling effective patient-level tabular-image representation learning for dementia detection. Experiments on OASIS-3 dataset show that the proposed framework achieves strong performance compared with representative approaches.</p></details> |  |
| **[EndoWorld-JEPA: Motion-Guided Global-Local Latent World Modeling for Endoscopic Video Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27890v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Endoscopic video contains state transitions induced by camera displacement, deformable tissue, and instrument interaction, yet existing pre-training methods treat dynamics implicitly. Inspired by action-conditioned joint-embedding predictive world models and by the observation that endoscopic optical flow contains both shared image-plane displacement and localized residual motion, we reformulate passive endoscopic video pre-training as causal motion-guided latent-state prediction. We present EndoWorld-JEPA, to our knowledge the first latent world-model pre-training framework designed for general-purpose endoscopic video representation learning. In place of recorded actions, calibrated poses, or robot kinematics, the model conditions prediction of the next latent state on motion observed up to the current state. Its Optical Motion-guided module separates dominant displacement from residual motion and converts the resulting evidence into an ordered temporal motion condition and spatial local-motion weights. Complementary global and motion-local predictors use these outputs: the temporal condition modulates both latent transitions, whereas the local weights define an auxiliary motion-local state that extends prediction from scene-level evolution to spatially selective dynamics. Patch-wise motion weighting emphasizes localized change while retaining anatomical context, and past-motion-window dropout randomly withholds the complete motion condition to reduce dependence on unreliable motion. This global--local formulation learns predictable visual--motion dynamics in representation space without reconstructing stochastic appearance artifacts. Evaluation shows that a common pre-trained checkpoint achieves strong transfer across four downstream benchmarks, supporting motion-guided global--local latent prediction as an effective pre-training paradigm for endoscopic video.</p></details> |  |
| **[Decoupled Representation Learning for Face Presentation Attack Detection under Smart Glass Capture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27800v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Face presentation attack detection is typically studied under fixed camera or handheld acquisition, whereas smart glass capture introduces a more challenging setting with ego motion, changing viewpoint, background clutter, distance variation, and less controlled illumination. To address this challenge, we present \emph{SmaGla-FPAD}, a smart glass based face presentation attack database acquired using Ray-Ban Meta Wayfarer Smart Glasses across indoor, outdoor, and in-car settings. The database contains 297,000 sample images with both bona fide and silicone mask based attacks. We further propose \emph{DeCoupPAD-FAS++}, a decoupled representation learning framework that separates spoof related evidence from nuisance sensitive capture variation through complementary attack and style branches, pseudo-domain supervision, adversarial suppression, and prompt guided semantic alignment. Experiments on SmaGla-FPAD show that the proposed method achieves the best balance between attack rejection and bona fide acceptance in both in domain and cross scenario evaluation. Ablation results further confirm the contribution of disentanglement, pseudo-domain supervision, and prompt based regularisation under smart glass capture.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted in IJCB 2026</p></details> |
| **[PanoGraphGS: Unifying Panoptic Segmentation and Hierarchical 3D Scene Graphs with Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27696v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D scene graphs support language-guided navigation by organizing physical entities and their containment and spatial relations into hierarchical maps. Building such structured maps requires a scene representation that consistently distinguishes semantic regions and object instances across viewpoints. To this end, we present PanoGraphGS, a unified framework that integrates panoptic 3D Gaussian representation learning with hierarchical 3D scene graph construction. The framework learns semantic and instance features on shared Gaussian geometry. Prototype-based semantic learning and association-based instance learning produce consistent panoptic predictions across viewpoints. The learned representation supports novel-view panoptic segmentation and direct Gaussian-level panoptic decoding. A semantic-aware scene graph module further recovers a floor-room-stuff-object hierarchy with containment and spatial relations. We further release NaviGS, a real-world dataset for panoramic scene understanding. The dataset provides 3DGS, mesh, RGB, semantic, and depth data. For 3D scene graph identification, PanoGraphGS outperforms the strongest evaluated baseline in object-node accuracy by more than 50\% points on average across Replica and NaviGS. PanoGraphGS also achieves state-of-the-art performance among the evaluated methods in 2D panoptic segmentation. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of PanoGraphGS in both hierarchical 3D scene understanding and 2D panoptic segmentation. The project website is available at: https://github.com/gaojh0333/PanoGraphGS.</p></details> | 9 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Learning Inter-Graph Interactions Between Heterogeneous Substructures of Chemical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27614v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Complex chemical systems containing heterogeneous substructures are common in real-world applications. Various physical phenomena of the complex chemical systems are derived from the interactions between the heterogeneous substructures. However, existing graph representation learning methods for inter-graph interactions assumed graph-level interactions between homogeneous structures, such as organic molecules and inorganic crystalline materials. We propose a data descriptor of the complex chemical systems and a graph neural network for learning inter-graph interactions between organic and inorganic compounds. We applied the proposed method to predict the physical properties of hybrid solar cell materials containing heterogeneous substructures, which have received significant attention for sustainable energy resources. By learning heterogeneous inter-graph interactions, the proposed method achieved state-of-the-art accuracy in predicting band gaps of 1,682 hybrid solar cell materials.</p></details> |  |
| **[Causal Spatiotemporal State Evolution for Monocular 4D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27594v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While feed-forward models excel in static 3D geometry estimation, extending them to dynamic 4D scenes requires modeling visual geometry as a causally evolving state. Current methods typically rely on unordered temporal aggregation, which lacks the directional structure of physical time. Furthermore, existing supervision often treats geometry and motion as disjoint objectives, failing to exploit their inherent physical coupling. Consequently, they lack a unified constraint to effectively supervise temporal state transitions, leading to inconsistent 4D scene representations. To address this, we propose a feed-forward framework for causal 4D visual geometry learning from monocular videos. At its core, Causal Spatiotemporal Mamba augments temporal aggregation with directional state propagation. Through complementary Temporal-Spatial and Spatial-Temporal Scans, CSM continuously propagates geometry-aware states across frames to model spatiotemporal evolution. To align this causal state evolution with observable dynamics, we introduce Unified Motion Supervision. Rather than treating motion as an independent objective, UMS leverages geometry-aware predictions from the camera and depth heads to construct a unified forward motion field that is consistent with the underlying 3D structure. This geometry-grounded supervision explicitly constrains inter-frame state transitions, enabling stable states propagation across frames. Extensive evaluations on dynamic benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across video depth, camera pose, and optical flow estimation. These results validate that the synergy between the directional state propagation of Causal Spatiotemporal Mamba and Unified Motion Supervision effectively captures continuous scene evolution, significantly reducing temporal drift and reinforcing spatial-temporal consistency in 4D representation learning.</p></details> |  |
| **[STEAM: Stable Self-Training with Elastic Matching and Adaptive Purification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09057v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cross-view geo-localization (CVGL) aims to perform visual localization by matching drone-view images with corresponding satellite images. Existing supervised methods rely on large-scale, manually annotated cross-view image pairs, which are costly and hard to scale. Many unsupervised approaches obtain pseudo-supervision through image-generation-based warm-up or clustering. These procedures may introduce distribution bias or propagate pseudo-label errors. To address these limitations, we propose STEAM (Stable Self-Training with Elastic Matching and Adaptive Purification), a direct self-training framework for unsupervised cross-view geo-localization. It combines end-to-end representation learning with iterative pseudo-label refinement without image-generation-based warm-up or clustering. Specifically, the Stable Spatial-Aware Module constructs image descriptors through bounded nonlinear spatial weighting. Elastic Matching combines Bidirectional Top-K Soft Matching with Dynamic Threshold Filtering to accommodate many-to-one cross-view correspondences while filtering ambiguous candidates. Adaptive Purification maintains the pseudo-label repository through Confidence-Aware Updates, Age-Aware Updates, and Expired Label Removal. These operations respectively update assignments through confidence comparisons, track consecutive epochs without agreement or successful replacement, and discard entries whose age reaches a preset limit. On University-1652, STEAM achieves the highest R@1 and AP among the compared unsupervised methods in both retrieval directions. It also achieves competitive performance on SUES-200 and DenseUAV.</p></details> |  |
| **[MiCLA: Mixture-to-Constituent Latent Alignment for Polyphonic Audio Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27491v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world audio typically contains concurrent sound events, yet standard self-supervised methods treat each clip as an isolated training unit without explicitly modeling mixture--constituent relationships. Stage 2 introduces bidirectional alignment: for a synthetic mixture, MiCLA suppresses the component aligned with one constituent and aligns the residual with the other, using projective residualization on patch tokens and sample-adaptive soft residualization on the global class token. Furthermore, a Joint Semantic Retrieval diagnostic shows that MiCLA improves simultaneous retrieval of both constituent classes over target-averaging baselines. These results indicate that directional mixture-to-constituent alignment provides an effective pre-training signal for polyphonic audio representation learning.</p></details> | 13 pages, 3 figures |
| **[Registration-Grounded Spectral Fusion for Unregistered WLI/NBI Endoscopic Lesion Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26395v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>White-light imaging (WLI) and narrow-band imaging (NBI) provide complementary cues for endoscopic lesion segmentation, yet their sequential acquisition often results in spatially inconsistent observations. The central challenge is not merely to establish cross-modal correspondence, but to determine how complementary information should be incorporated when such correspondence is uncertain. To address this issue, we propose a correspondence-conditioned, modality-asymmetric complex spectral fusion framework for paired-but-unregistered WLI/NBI segmentation. The framework first establishes bidirectional feature correspondence through topology-regularized registration and derives local reliability from post-warping feature discrepancies. Rather than simply reweighting fused features, the estimated reliability directly controls the construction of modality-asymmetric polar representations, where WLI-derived cues primarily parameterize appearance-related moduli and NBI-derived cues parameterize structure-sensitive angular components. The resulting complex representations are further refined through Fourier-domain recalibration with reliability-biased residual reconstruction. Experiments on FAHSYSU and PICCOLO demonstrate competitive improvements over existing segmentation methods. Module ablations, modality-role reversal, and reliability analyses further support the effectiveness of correspondence-conditioned polar representation learning.</p></details> | 15 pages |
| **[Where to Look Matters: Rethinking Sub-Volume Sampling in 3D Medical Self-supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13915v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-supervised learning on 3D medical images commonly relies on random sub-volume sampling during pretraining. To this end, we first show through controlled crop-quality manipulations that better sub-volumes lead to better downstream transfer, revealing the observation policy as an overlooked bottleneck in 3D medical SSL. Motivated by this finding, we revisit sub-volume sampling as a where-to-look problem. We propose VolumeProbe, a plug-and-play framework that replaces random cropping with an offline coarse-to-fine probe bank guided by anatomy-awareness, informativeness, and diversity. We instantiate VolumeProbe as a lightweight Lite variant using geometric and image-statistical cues, and a diagnostic Feat variant using frozen visual features, to examine whether lightweight volume-intrinsic priors suffice or richer semantic cues reliably provide additional value. Extensive experiments across multiple 3D medical SSL backbones and downstream tasks demonstrate that VolumeProbe-Lite consistently improves transfer performance over random sampling. Mechanistic analyses further link these gains to more anatomically plausible and informative observations, reduced spatial redundancy, more efficient learning dynamics, and more structured representations.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026. Project page: https://volumeprobe.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Representing short-term movement to reveal animal identity and learning progress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27140v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How learning progresses over time and varies across individuals is a longstanding question in cognitive and behavioral science. However, a major challenge is identifying observable behavioral measures that faithfully capture learning progression. Existing approaches commonly rely on predefined behavioral features or task-level performance, while fine-scale posture and movement in freely behaving animals remain less explored. In this paper, we ask: can short-term movement trajectories reveal learning progress and individual identity? We introduce a self-supervised representation learning framework built on a simple hypothesis: behavioral characteristics associated with identity and learning remain relatively stable over nearby time intervals. Using temporal proximity as supervision, our framework learns compact representations directly from movement trajectories without manually defining behavioral states or kinematic features. Using behavior data from freely behaving rats, we show that the learned representations not only improve prediction of identity and learning-related readouts over existing methods, but also provide a tool for investigating broader scientific questions about when and how these signals appear in behavior. Specifically, trajectories as short as one second contain informative learning and identity signals. Egocentric pose dynamics are more predictive of identity, whereas allocentric motion is more predictive of learning-related variation, and both signals are strongest at decision junctions in the alternation cohort. We further use a representation-conditioned diffusion model to inspect movement patterns associated with identity and learning progress. Our findings establish short-term movement as an informative behavioral "fingerprint" for studying learning and individual differences. Our code is available at https://github.com/pizhn/TRAIL.</p></details> | <details><summary>The f...</summary><p>The first three authors contribute equally to this work</p></details> |
| **[SpecMRL: Redesign Matryoshka Representation Learning within Parametric Spectrum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26824v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) is designed to support flexible representation sizes by jointly optimizing nested prefixes. While effective, this design lacks an explicit constraint to ensure that the leading portion provides a suitable compact encoding. Specifically, we extend MRL with a parameter-derived spectral basis organized with feature importance. SpecMRL otherwise follows standard MRL, with prefix readout performed in the learned spectral space. Experiments on MTEB show that SpecMRL outperforms MRL at all evaluated reduced dimensions, with larger gains under more aggressive compression. These results suggest that the learned spectral basis better prioritizes informative directions, enabling more effective representation compression. Notably, SpecMRL extends the performance frontier of MRL, surpassing full-dimensional MRL with only half the embedding dimensions.</p></details> | preprint |
| **[TriFold: Folding irregular 3D Gaussians into structured latents for 3D Scene Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23050v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent progress in 3D generation has motivated growing interest in compact generative representations of complex 3D scenes. For native 3D Gaussian scenes, however, learning such representations remains challenging due to widely varying primitive counts, nonuniform spatial density, and differences in geometry and appearance even among spatially neighboring primitives. In this work, we introduce TriFold, a native 3D Gaussian VAE that encodes Gaussian scenes into a fixed-layout triplane latent and jointly reconstructs their geometry and appearance. The key insight is to impose structural regularity on the latent space while preserving the spatial adaptivity of the underlying Gaussians. During encoding, we use cell-wise learned queries to aggregate projected Gaussians into multiple slots per cell, reducing the information loss caused by single-feature pooling. During decoding, occupancy prediction identifies active regions, where query-based set prediction and candidate selection reconstruct local Gaussian sets with spatially varying primitive counts. Building on this representation, we train a latent flow-matching model for conditional scene-level 3DGS generation, enabling point-cloud-conditioned synthesis and single-image-conditioned amodal generation. Experiments demonstrate that TriFold achieves high-fidelity reconstruction and generation from a single image that provides only a partial observation of the scene, validating its effectiveness for native 3D Gaussian representation learning. https://fantasy-amap.github.io/TriFold</p></details> | <details><summary>30pag...</summary><p>30pages, Project page: https://fantasy-amap.github.io/TriFold</p></details> |
| **[Hierarchical Token Merging with Recursive Autoencoders for Unsupervised Grammar Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26695v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hierarchical abstraction is a fundamental principle across many data modalities, enabling complex sequences to be represented compositionally at multiple levels. Learning such hierarchies directly from data, without explicit structural supervision, remains a central challenge in representation learning. While autoencoders learn compressed representations through reconstruction, this objective does not necessarily favour meaningful compositional structure. We study this challenge through unsupervised grammar induction, where the underlying hierarchy can be evaluated explicitly. We introduce a hierarchical token-merging autoencoder that recursively combines adjacent tokens into increasingly abstract representations, compressing entire sequences through a variational information bottleneck. The model jointly learns representations and hierarchical structure by marginalising over possible binary trees using dynamic programming. To address training instability, we combine independently trained models through unsupervised consensus and distillation. Experiments on a synthetic, ambiguous grammar demonstrate the emergence of meaningful hierarchical structure without structural supervision. The resulting model achieves 93% grammatical parse-validity and 93% exact reconstruction on held-out sequences, compared with 42% parse-validity for the strongest trivial baseline. These findings establish a proof of concept for hierarchical token merging as a mechanism for unsupervised structure discovery and abstraction learning.</p></details> |  |
| **[Global Time-Aware Conditioning (GTC): A Mechanism for Time-Aware Pediatric Sleep Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26618v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sleep physiology evolves throughout the night, with changes in sleep cycles, respiratory stability, and cardiovascular dynamics. On open-ended conditional generation, full MLM adaptation reaches BLEU/ROUGE-L of 0.163/0.583 on QQP and 0.107/0.327 on Quasar-T, placing it in the same broad performance range as published BERT models trained through diffusion without explicit diffusion training. | **[Learning Geometry-Aware Planning Representations for Monocular Flight with Cross-Modal Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26508v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing high-speed navigation systems for micro aerial vehicles (MAVs) commonly rely on depth cameras, stereo vision, or LiDAR to obtain explicit geometric information, while monocular cameras offer a lighter and lower-cost alternative. | **[Brain-to-Language Decoding: Paradigms, Signals, Methods, Evaluation, Practical Use and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27650v5)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Brain-to-language decoding translates neural activity associated with language production, internal speech and perception into linguistic or expressive outputs. The synthesis identifies complementary routes to progress: phonetic, acoustic and semantic targets preserve different aspects of a message; shared representations support reuse across recording conditions and tasks; and online communication increasingly depends on calibration, feedback and user control alongside decoding accuracy. Shared benchmarks enable algorithmic comparisons, while longitudinal studies reveal the demands of sustained use. | **[ORBIT: Autoregressive Time Series Generation via Next Frequency-Band Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27000v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep generative models provide an effective modern solution to alleviating the problem of data scarcity by synthesizing realistic time series. | **[REST: Reliability-Guided Spatial Transcriptomics Supervision for Gene Expression Prediction from Histopathological HE-Stained Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26988v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Histopathology is central to cancer diagnosis, but morphology in Hematoxylin and Eosin (HE)-stained images does not directly reveal the molecular states that characterize disease heterogeneity and inform tumor characterization, prognostic assessment, and biomarker discovery. ## Causal Inference
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Estimating Treatment Effects in Complex Disease Dynamics: Immortal Time, Varying Hazards, and Multiple Time Scales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35678v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In observational survival studies, delayed treatment initiation can induce immortal time bias in effect estimation when treatment groups are defined by future uptake. Despite originating from distinct predictive or causal inference paradigms, time-dependent approaches (e.g. the time-dependent Cox model, landmark supermodel, multi-state model, and g-formula) are often treated as interchangeable in applied research when addressing immortal time bias. These models effectively reclassify person-time according to time-varying exposure, yet the interpretation of their target estimands depends on assumptions about the underlying disease process and clinically relevant time scales. We study effect estimation for allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation under baseline intention-to-transplant strategies in acute myeloid leukemia (i.e. transplantation is planned for a prespecified time, subject to the patient's eligibility), within a multi-state framework allowing time-inhomogeneous Markov, semi-Markov, and state arrival extended semi-Markov dynamics. Using target trial emulation, we benchmark, on the probability scale, estimates derived from Cox-type time-dependent models and the g-formula against the corresponding theoretical values implied by the stochastic data-generating process. Decomposing the partial likelihood score, we show that single-time-scale models can be systematically biased when death intensities from different originating states are not proportional on a common time scale, as can occur when the clock is reset after intermediate events. We quantify these findings in a phase III simulation study and illustrate their implications in an application to data from three acute myeloid leukemia trials.</p></details> | <details><summary>34 pa...</summary><p>34 pages, 10 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[A Hybrid Framework for Explainable Aviation Risk: Causal Chain Extraction from Safety Reports with Physics-Based Failure Quantification and Interventional Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35658v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aviation authorities hold decades of accident reports whose narratives record how investigators reconstructed each accident. In this work, we propose a hybrid framework in which a text-based corpus identifies the leading factors of a failure mode. Physics-based models then quantify how likely that failure is at a stated operating point. Ordered causal chains extracted from 56,202 American accident reports with a local large language model aggregate into a causation knowledge graph. On an evaluation sample of 2,000 reports, the chains recover 90.8% of investigator-flagged causes. Each physical factor whose drivers the record carries receives an occurrence model calibrated to the accident population. A leaky noisy-OR fitted to the corpus combines the factors into a failure mode probability that supports intervention. For engine power loss, the carburetor icing model separates 1,205 icing accidents from other accidents using temperature and dewpoint alone, reaching 0.61 in area under the receiver operating characteristic curve against a density ratio ceiling of 0.64. Routine weather hours give no valid control, because flying concentrates in fair weather. On later years the physics adds 0.03 to that area for occurrence and nothing for severity, so its value lies in interpretability. On four named accidents, abduction gives a probability of necessity of 0.29 to 0.50 for the heat omission, under monotonicity. The Fast Causal Inference algorithm does not license the largest backdoor adjustment on the learned networks.</p></details> |  |
| **[CausalMix: Data Mixture as Causal Inference for Language Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01104v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In Large Language Model (LLM) training, data mixing plays a pivotal role in determining model performance. Recent methods optimize mixture weights via proxy models, but they rely on the assumption of static data distributions. This limitation restricts their ability to scale seamlessly from small settings to larger data pools and model sizes. In this paper, we propose CausalMix to address this limitation by casting data mixture optimization as a causal inference problem. We formulate the statistical features of the data pool as covariates and the domain mixture as the treatment. Furthermore, we successfully generalize the framework to long chain-of-thought data on Qwen3-4B-Base. By leveraging causal modeling to isolate confounding biases, CausalMix infers state-dependent data mixtures offline, with the selected mixture fixed during each training run. Extensive experiments show that the mixture guided by CausalMix achieves competitive performance across downstream tasks compared with other baselines. The CATE Interpreter visualizes the learned mixing strategy. We lift this mean from a sample statistic to a functional of the data-generating law and prove that it is well defined under explicit geometric assumptions. Because it is not linear, it yields three causal estimands instead of one: populational, individual and local. We identify the populational estimand by inverse-probability weighting and the local estimand through the conditional outcome laws, estimate them by weighting and by matching, and show that the individual one is not identifiable. On simulations with timing variability or heterogeneous effect shapes, our estimators recover the shape that the FATE loses. Extended journal version of our NeurIPS 2024 paper. Chenxi Liu and Yongqiang Chen contributed equally. 95 pages, 52 figures. Rather than any single observation, such as an IoC (Indicator of Compromise), it is the arrangement that becomes a detection, or intelligence, mechanism. This is based on an intelligence analysis technique called Analysis of Competing Hypotheses (ACH), which the design lifts from the limitation of a few hypotheses at a time, to thousands, and then also operationalizes it for continuous detection purposes. Language models make the concept, and scale, practical: they turn prose reports into brains, judge matches, and drive the loop that keeps brains current. A partial pattern in a brain can also match a new value, such as a fresh C2 (command and control) domain name, assisting with the discovery of new indicators, and turning unknown unknowns into known unknowns, and potentially into knowns. The author would like to thank Ryan Moon for the collaboration, and Sounil Yu, Chris Inglis, Thomas Roccia, Marius Benthin, Israel Grimberg, Michal Kamensky, Justin Borland, Brandon Levene, John Hultquist, and John Bambenek for their feedback and assistance.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agentic Algorithm Design: LLMs that Propose, Formalise, and Prove Runtime Bounds for Evolutionary Algorithms in Lean](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35595v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs now prove hard theorems in Lean. Can they also design algorithms and prove their correctness and efficiency? LLM-guided design of optimisation heuristics (such as AlphaEvolve) is purely empirical. Automated theorem provers with LLMs prove given statements. So far, there has been little evidence that LLMs can do both: design an algorithm and guarantee its efficiency by a mathematical proof. We present a staged pipeline modelled after research methodology in design and analysis of evolutionary algorithms. The agent proposes black-box optimisation algorithms, and Lean guards filter out flawed designs. The agent then defines a potential function and proves drift bounds that guarantee an expected runtime improvement over the baseline algorithm. At no point in the pipeline are the algorithms compared empirically. The agent generates a black-box optimisation algorithm with expected runtime $O(n^2)$ on a CycleUncover problem, where the classical (1+1) EA needs runtime $e^{Ω(n)}$. The pipeline also designs and analyses a black-box optimisation algorithm for integer-valued submodular minimisation with expected runtime $O(n^{2+2c})$, and it matches the optimal order $O(n^2)$ on LeadingOnes. Across 8 problem classes, 12 of 15 runs with frontier models complete the pipeline with a machine-checked expected-runtime bound. An ablation study shows that removing the staging and asking the agent to zero-shot an algorithmic design with a runtime guarantee leads to a copy of the seeded baseline algorithm (random search) or to incomplete proofs. | **[Context Is Not Free: Carbon-Aware LLM Routing for Multi-Turn LLM Web Services across Geo-Distributed Data Centers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35230v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The sustainability of the Web has drawn growing attention, as large language model (LLM)-based web services leave a substantial carbon footprint. On the Security and Trustworthiness of Fully Transparent LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35199v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>There are different levels of transparency for LLMs: open weights, open data, and/or open pipeline. Our core findings are that, due to the limited reproducibility of LLM pipelines, in practice, users cannot verify the correspondence between open weights and the associated open data and pipeline. Thus, in order to trust a model, users still need to trust that developers acted honestly, reducing the benefits over models that provide less "transparency". Moreover, we find that even when LLMs are fully transparent and trusted, this openness can favor adversaries. Open weights in particular, but also open data and pipelines, can increase the success rate of attacks. Although the same mechanisms can be used to improve the model and protect it against attacks, the current lack of robust lightweight mechanisms to patch LLM vulnerabilities limits the benefits of openness at this time. We study how two of them, Proptest-AI and Agentic-PBT, generalize to ten programs in five domains: deep learning libraries, databases, system software, compilers, and LLM serving engines. Most of these programs take text in their own language, such as queries, configurations, and programs. Agentic-PBT covers more branches than Proptest-AI on seven of the ten programs with Opus 5 and on all ten with GLM-5.3-Flash. Domain-specific fuzzers cover more of the compilers and of PostgreSQL and less of PyTorch than Agentic-PBT. We filed 180 bug reports, 44 of which duplicate reports that others filed after the models' knowledge cutoffs; maintainers have confirmed 65 of the others, in all five domains, and fixed 23. We draw lessons for building and evaluating such writers: low observability hurts them, and mature fuzzers paired with LLM-written properties could check that each feature behaves as documented.</p></details> |  |
| **[Broken on Arrival: Non-Functional Quantized LLM Artifacts in Public Model Registries and How to Catch Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05881v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Developers download quantized large language models (LLMs) from public registries to run them locally. A 15-task code screen identified suspects, which we retested on all 164 HumanEval+ tasks. Seven official tags (2.3%) solved at most six of them. All five registry files matched our uncalibrated rebuilds tensor for tensor, so none was corrupted. Exact reproducibility did not imply function. With the weights, toolchain and tensor mix held fixed, adding an importance matrix raised three Qwen2.5-Coder-3B builds from zero to 76-113 solved tasks. The same uncalibrated recipe left the smaller Qwen2.5-Coder-1.5B functional, showing that the recipe alone does not predict failure. None of 66 sampled tags that passed the screen was non-functional. Census, attribution method, scoring and conclusions revised; title subtitle updated. Code and data: https://github.com/aditi-p31/quantcheck (release v3.3)</p></details> |
| **[Mathematical Reasoning via Intervention-Based Time-Series Causal Discovery Using LLMs as Concept Mastery Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07600v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent methods for improving LLM mathematical reasoning, whether through MCTS-based test-time search or causal graph-guided knowledge injection, cannot identify which concepts causally contribute to a correct answer, as the observed association may be spurious, driven by confounders such as problem difficulty. Because the intervention exogenously sets the concept state independently of problem difficulty, ICP separates confounding that observational methods cannot. On 67 screened problems, the ICP of the top-ranked concept (+0.219) is significantly larger than that of the negative control (+0.039; paired $t$-test, $p < 10^{-6}$, Cohen's $d = 0.86$), confirming that the probe discriminates causally relevant concepts from irrelevant ones. Analysis of 601 Omni-MATH problems further shows that solved problems have 6.1$\times$ higher ATE than unsolved ones (0.338 vs. 0.055), confirming that ICP is predictive of problem-solving success. With a 7B-parameter LLM whose weights are entirely frozen, CIKA achieves 69.7\% on the contamination-free Omni-MATH-Rule benchmark and 64.0\% overall, compared to 60.5\% for o1-mini, and 97.2\% on GSM8K, 46--50\% on AIME 2024--2026, and 46.2\% on MathArena. The Causal Knowledge Activation component contributes 33.8\% of correct answers on problems where the base model alone fails, demonstrating that the LLM already possessed but had not activated the requisite knowledge.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 0 figures. v2: Revised as a technical report. | **[The Illusion of Reasoning: Exposing Evasive Data Contamination in LLMs via Zero-CoT Truncation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21856v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive reasoning abilities across a wide range of tasks, but data contamination undermines the objective evaluation of these capabilities. Current approaches struggle to reliably detect such stealthy contamination. In this work, we uncover a critical phenomenon: a model's generated reasoning steps actively mask its underlying memorization. Inspired by this, we propose the Zero-CoT Probe (ZCP), a novel black-box detection method that deliberately truncates the entire Chain-of-Thought (CoT) process to expose latent shortcut mappings. To further isolate memorization from the model's intrinsic problem-solving capabilities, ZCP compares the model's zero-CoT performance on the original benchmark against an isomorphically perturbed reference dataset. Furthermore, we introduce Contamination Confidence, a metric that quantifies both the likelihood and severity of contamination, moving beyond simple binary classifications. Extensive experiments on both previously identified contaminated models and specially fine-tuned contaminated models demonstrate that ZCP robustly detects both direct and evasive data contamination. The code for ZCP is accessible at https://github.com/Yifan-Lan/zero-cot-probe.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 5 figures, 25 tables</p></details> |
| **[LLMs are not stochastic parrots: Evidence for meaning-mediated abstraction from conlang-like tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34187v4)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The strong version of the stochastic parrot argument claims that, although large language models (LLMs) may exceed rote regurgitation, they cannot move beyond statistical pattern matching into abstraction or reasoning, remaining ontologically near the lower bound of pattern reuse despite producing alluringly fluent text. We test this hypothesis using conlang-like tasks. Several LLMs are given only natural-language descriptions of fictional languages that subvert prominent superficial patterns in training data by combining statistically uncommon and unattested features. Crucially, no example outputs are given. We argue that if the models exhibit rule-following behaviour, they cannot be relying solely on superficial statistical patterns; such patterns often work against the correct output. Instead, successful performance requires representations of the constraints specified in the prompt. Across three complementary task families, models systematically move in the meaning-predicted direction: they distinguish prompt exposure from instructed use, alter semantic relationships in response to novel constraints, and sometimes produce exact matches to complex translation answer keys. Although performance varies across the spectrum of models used, these results provide evidence for meaning-mediated abstraction in LLMs and refute the strong stochastic parrot hypothesis. Our work shows that, under appropriate architectural and contextual constraints, statistical learning can produce meaning-mediated abstractions, although generation remains strongly constrained by superficial plausibility. We discuss implications for model development and for understanding how increasingly abstract representations may emerge from plausible-text-generation objectives.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prese...</summary><p>Presented as a poster at ISC Summer School 2026; related work presented at IC2S2 2026; a synthesis of this project and related work presented at SLSA/ 4S 2026</p></details> |

Source code: https://github.com/Janghyun1230/FastKVzip</p></details> |
| **[Same Signal, Opposite Meaning: Direction-Informed Adaptive Learning for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06908v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive test-time compute for LLM agents aims to invoke extra computation only when it improves performance. This makes gating a utility-calibration problem: gating signals should align with whether extra computation improves the final outcome over the base policy. We show that this alignment is unstable: the same signal predicts rollout benefit in one setting and rollout harm in another, with reversals across environments and backbones even when the task is fixed. Wrong-direction gates can therefore worsen performance by precisely selecting harmful states. This reversal reflects a deeper distinction between compute need and compute suitability: a high uncertainty signal may indicate decision-difficult states where rollouts help compare alternatives, or intervention-unsuitable states where the current context does not support useful rollout-based improvement. Under this two-source model, fixed-direction gates are unreliable across heterogeneous settings. To address this, we propose DIAL (Direction-Informed Adaptive Learning), a sparse gate trained from signal-agnostic counterfactual exploration to learn the utility direction of state features per (environment, backbone). Across six environments and three backbones, DIAL yields a stronger overall success-cost trade-off than fixed-direction baselines. Our code is open-sourced at https://github.com/zmli6/DIAL.</p></details> | <details><summary>Agent...</summary><p>Agent, Test time learing, Accpeted by NeuIPS 2026</p></details> |

To improve RL for moderate-sized LLMs, we introduce a graph-aware curriculum that schedules training by the structural difficulty of questions using an easy-to-hard biased sampler. We evaluate GraphDancer on the multi-domain GRBench benchmark, training only on Academic and Biomedical graphs. Testing covers held-out questions from these domains as well as unseen E-commerce, Literature, and Legal graphs. With either Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct or Qwen3-14B as the backbone, GraphDancer outperforms graph-agent baselines using the same backbone across all evaluation domains. These results indicate that the learned graph exploration and reasoning capabilities transfer across domains. On average across unseen domains, GraphDancer with a 14B backbone also outperforms baselines powered by the closed-source models GPT-4.1-mini and GPT-5-nano. Trajectory analyses further show that GraphDancer retrieves answer-bearing evidence more often and achieves higher accuracy when such evidence is found, while substantially reducing invalid-protocol outcomes.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages. | **[Developer Task Delegation to LLMs and the Trust Factor: A Mixed Methods Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34317v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the rise of Large Language Models (LLMs), software developers are presented with a powerful new approach: delegating software development tasks to LLMs. Existing studies have explored task delegation for a subset of tasks and factors. We explore these questions through 22 interviews and a survey with 160 software practitioners. Drawing on our qualitative findings, we discuss the rationales behind these choices and explore how task difficulty, task risk, developer motivation, and trust appear to be associated with software development task delegation to LLMs. Our statistical analysis reveals that trust is a significant factor associated with task delegation to LLMs. Finally, we share actionable implications for human-AI collaboration in software research, and for software practitioners to make the most of their newly found powers of delegation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in the Proceedings of the 49th IEEE/ACM International Conference on Software Engineering (ICSE'27)</p></details> |
| **[A Stratified Multi-Rater Evaluation of LLM-Based Virtual Standardized Patients with a Deployed Data-Generation Platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29312v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Objective Structured Clinical Examination (OSCE) is widely used to assess clinical communication skills in medical education, requiring trainees to take histories and reason clinically with standardized patients (SPs). Large language model (LLM)-based virtual standardized patients (VSPs), typically built by persona-injected prompting, offer a practical alternative. We evaluated four LLMs (GPT-4o, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, Llama-3.1 70B, and HyperCLOVA X), spanning commercial, open-source and Korean-developed models, against a human-expert reference across three Korean OSCE cases (hematuria, fever in pregnancy, and chronic cough). In a double-blind, fully crossed design, 17 senior medical-student raters scored every model utterance on three sentence-level and six encounter-level Likert metrics, yielding 7,480 data points. We analyzed the scores using a stratified linear mixed-effects model based on an 11-category history-taking and 3-level linguistic-form taxonomy. At the encounter level, three of the four LLMs fell 0.08 to 0.47 Likert points below the human-expert reference on Comprehensibility, Consistency and Hallucination-freeness. The difference was not statistically significant for Hallucination-freeness in Claude 3.5 Sonnet and GPT-4o, Consistency in GPT-4o, and Engagement in HyperCLOVA X. Llama-3.1 70B fell significantly below the reference on all six metrics. | **[KV Packet: Recomputation-Free Context-Independent KV Caching for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13226v3)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) rely heavily on Key-Value (KV) caching to minimize inference latency. In this paper, we propose KV Packet, a recomputation-free cache reuse framework that treats cached documents as immutable "packets" wrapped in light-weight trainable soft-token adapters, which are trained via self-supervised distillation to bridge context discontinuities. Experiments on Llama-3.1 and Qwen-3 demonstrate that the proposed KV Packet method achieves near-zero FLOPs and lower TTFT than recomputation-based baselines, while retaining F1 scores comparable to those of the full recomputation baseline. | **[Repairing Broken Access Control in Web Applications with LLMs: An Empirical Benchmark Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34290v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Broken access control (BAC) ranks among the most severe web-application vulnerability classes, yet its automated repair remains largely unexplored. Unlike memory-safety vulnerabilities, BAC is a logic vulnerability whose repair demands a deep understanding of an application's access-control model and business semantics, which traditional automated vulnerability repair techniques handle poorly. Large language models (LLMs) open a promising avenue for this problem, but no standard benchmark exists for studying how well they repair BAC vulnerabilities. We construct BAC-Bench, the first benchmark for BAC repair, consisting of 46 real-world BAC vulnerabilities drawn from six widely used web applications spanning four programming languages. Each vulnerability is equipped with a sandboxed runtime environment, a PoC validation program and a functional test program. We further propose a BAC repair-strategy framework that characterizes any BAC patch along four dimensions, Position, Coverage, Mechanism, and Content. Evaluating eight base LLMs and two commercial LLM agents on BAC-Bench, we find that single-turn base LLMs repair 37.68% of the vulnerabilities on average, while two minimal scaffolds, Apply-Feedback and Navigate-and-Locate, raise this average to 66.03%, with the best base LLM reaching 75.36%, close to the 78.99% of the best agent. Notably, some LLM-generated patches surpass the official patches, and one incidentally closed a zero-day vulnerability that had lain dormant for three years. Our findings delineate the current capability boundary of LLM-based BAC repair and offer guidance toward its practical adoption.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Russian, a Brazilian, and a German Walk into a Bar, Ask the Same Question-- And Get Three Different Answers: Profiling LLM Perspective Diversity on Contested Topics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34278v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models(LLMs) have shown strong performance on tasks with verifiable answers, but many real-world queries involve subjectivity, disagreement, and conflicting evidence. | **[Evaluating LLM-based Agents for Multi-Turn Conversations: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.22458v3)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This survey examines evaluation methods for large language model (LLM)-based agents in multi-turn conversational settings. These components ensure that the performance of conversational agents is assessed in a holistic and meaningful manner. The second taxonomy system focuses on the evaluation methodologies. | **[Honesty Testing of LLMs and AI Agents: A Vision for Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34239v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human oversight of large language models (LLMs) and artificial intelligence (AI) agents increasingly depends on their reports. In this vision paper, we propose honesty testing of LLMs and AI agents as a new research area in software engineering. Building on existing honesty evaluations, our goal is to make faithful reporting a software quality requirement that developers can specify, test, and maintain. We define honesty bugs as behavioural defects in which an LLM or AI agent, when required to communicate faithfully, makes an explicit claim that conflicts with its own judgment at the time of reporting. We present an initial framework covering LLM-level testing under controlled inputs and agent-level testing of complete systems. The framework connects these levels to testing workflows and oracle evidence. We identify research directions in benchmarks, model and harness analysis, oracle construction, and lifecycle testing to support effective oversight alongside task correctness.</p></details> | 5 pages, 1 figure |
| **[VerifyMAS: Hypothesis Verification for Failure Attribution in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17467v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model-driven multi-agent systems (LLM-MAS) excel at complex tasks, yet unreliable agents remain a key bottleneck to system-level reliability. To address these challenges, we propose VerifyMAS, a hypothesis verification framework for agent failure attribution. This verification-based approach decomposes attribution into trajectory-level error validation and fine-grained agent localization, providing an error-first attribution approach that captures global failure patterns while substantially reducing the search space. We further introduce a hypothesis-based data construction strategy grounded in a structured error taxonomy and fine-tune a specialized LLM verifier model for trajectory-level failure verification and agent attribution. Experiments on Aegis-Bench and Who&When show that VerifyMAS consistently improves diverse backbone models, including open-source Qwen and API-based GPT models, outperforming prior methods without sacrificing inference efficiency for long multi-agent trajectories.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS2026</p></details> |

| **[Bug Severity Classification with Fine-Tuned Open LLMs: A Paired Comparison with TF-IDF and CodeBERT Baselines on Eclipse Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34127v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open large language models (LLMs) small enough to run on a laptop are an obvious candidate for automatic bug severity prediction. We measure what they add over cheaper classifiers on one task: assigning one of four Bugzilla severity levels (Critical, Major, Minor, Trivial) to Eclipse bug reports. From a public dump of 361,006 unique Eclipse reports we draw a class-balanced sample of 20,000 rows and compare eleven configurations of nine model types: six TF-IDF classifiers, a fine-tuned CodeBERT encoder, and Llama 3 8B and Qwen 2.5 7B, each run zero-shot and after QLoRA fine-tuning. All eleven are scored on the same 2,000 held-out reports, with bootstrap intervals and McNemar tests. The zero-shot runs reach macro-F1 0.305 and 0.321, below every TF-IDF model (0.430-0.489). The fine-tuned runs reach 0.526 and 0.485, but the zero-shot and fine-tuned setups also differ in checkpoint, serving runtime and decoding, so this gap cannot be credited to fine-tuning alone. Fine-tuned Llama 3 shows no detectable difference from CodeBERT (0.518; difference +0.007, 95% CI -0.017 to +0.032), and fine-tuned Qwen 2.5 is below it on macro-F1. The models differ more by class than in aggregate: fine-tuned Llama 3 finds 36% of Critical reports, Qwen 2.5 finds 69% but only 21% of Minor ones. The dump repeats 60,000 rows, and 8% of the test reports duplicate a training report; removing them lowers TF-IDF scores by up to 0.031 and leaves the LLMs almost unchanged. We release split identifiers, per-report predictions and the analysis script at https://github.com/tbayan/bug_finetuning_llm_artifacts, together with CyberTriage, a tool that serves the fine-tuned classifier locally.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 5 figures, 8 tables. Code and data: https://github.com/tbayan/bug_finetuning_llm_artifacts</p></details> |
| **[Human-LLM Alignment in Language Attitudes Toward Non-Native Japanese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01629v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly evaluate human writing in high-stakes domains such as hiring and academic assessment, putting non-native speakers at particular risk. | **[Automatic Configuration of LLM Post-Training Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18773v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM post-training pipelines that combine supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning are difficult to configure under realistic compute budgets: the configuration space is high-dimensional and heterogeneous, stages are strongly coupled, and each end-to-end evaluation is expensive. | **[Terminus-4B: Can a Smaller Model Replace Frontier LLMs at Agentic Execution Tasks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03195v3)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern coding agents increasingly delegate specialized subtasks to subagents, which are smaller, focused agentic loops that handle narrow responsibilities like search, debugging or terminal execution.
